I know it really matters and that my job depends on getting it right, but I still keep missing the finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks, or do you get easily distracted while working on something that requires sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I start my shift really trying to stay focused, after a while everything kind of blends together—the cutting, weighing, checking labels. I’ll start out really trying to stay locked in, but after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels kind of run together. It slows me down and makes me feel like I’m not fully there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused during an activity that wasn’t repetitive or routine—like a conversation, a meeting, or something new—and how did you handle that?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a non-routine example, but honestly the main thing I notice is still when I’m at work and it takes steady attention. Even when I start my shift really trying to stay focused, after a while everything kind of blends together—the cutting, weighing, checking labels. I’ll catch myself just staring at the same tray or the log sheet and not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose track of time or find yourself forgetting what you were supposed to do next while performing these tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens a lot. I’ll start out really trying to stay locked in, but after a while the cutting and weighing and checking labels kind of run together. Then I realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the log sheet and I’m not actually processing what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself losing focus or spacing out during activities outside of work, like reading, watching TV, or having conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s like I’ll start something trying really hard to pay attention, and then after a while it all kind of blurs together. At work it’s the cutting and weighing and checking labels, and I’ll realize I’ve just been staring at the same tray or the log sheet without really taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time outside of work when you had trouble staying focused—like when you were reading, watching TV, or talking with someone—and how did that affect you?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I guess it’s similar, but the clearest example is still when I’m doing something repetitive that needs attention. I’ll start out really trying to stay focused, like telling myself to just follow the steps, but after a while everything kind of blends together. I’ll realize I’ve been staring at the same thing—like the tray or the log sheet—and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. It slows me down and makes me feel like I’m not fully there.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused during an activity that wasn’t repetitive or routine—like a conversation, a meeting, or something new—and how did you handle that?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a non-routine example, but honestly the main thing I notice is still when I’m at work and it takes steady attention. Then I realize I’ve just been staring at the same tray or the log sheet and not really taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble staying focused during something completely new or unexpected—like learning a new task, having a deep conversation, or making a decision—and how that felt for you?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for something new or unexpected, but the example I can really point to is still at work. Then I’ll notice I’ve been staring at the same tray or the log sheet and not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next. It feels frustrating, like my brain just stops holding onto the task even though I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall a time outside of your usual work routine—like learning something new, having a complex conversation, or making an important decision—when you had trouble staying focused or found your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re asking about outside work, but I’m honestly not recalling a clear example like that right now.
